You'll be the product manager who finds that out. You'll talk to customers, use the product in real AI-assisted workflows, test ideas quickly with our designer and engineers, and build the measurement that tells us what's working. #7368. TikTok UK publishes live shopping report

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

TikTok says that almost one-third of live viewers have eventually gone on to buy a displayed product in-store.

TikTok UK published its first Live Shopping Report last week, which provides insight into how TikTok is becoming a more prominent retail destination in the U.K., and how livestreaming is changing the game for sellers.

The 20-page report looks at top product categories for live shopping and includes a range of notes on the increasing value of live shopping,

TikTok said that live shopping in the U.K. is on the rise, and has shared a range of engagement stats to underline this:

The guide includes overviews of the most popular live shopping categories, such as vertical-specific trend data and case study notes.

The most popular product categories driving TikTok sales in the U.K. are beauty, fashion, fast-moving consumer goods, pre-loved products, electronics, home and living and books and collectibles.

The breakdowns in the guide provide trend notes on all of these sectors, and could help marketers map out a more effective approach in the app.

TikTok is still trying to make in-stream shopping a bigger element, following the success that it had with in-app shopping in the China-based version of the app, called Douyin.

And in the U.K. at least, TikTok live shopping does appear to be catching on, albeit at a slower pace than what TikTok’s management would like.

Last year, TikTok Shops in the U.K. reported 60% year-over-year growth for the beauty category, making the app the fourth-largest beauty retailer in the country.

TikTok still has a way to go to convince more users to merge their shopping and social media engagement activities into a single app, but the company is making steady progress at driving more in-stream browsing and buying.

#7366. YouTube’s Clips Tool Turns Podcast Episodes Into Shorts via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

YouTube expanded its Studio video clips tool to Shorts, letting creators turn segments of long-form videos, like podcast episodes, into vertical Shorts.

YouTube has expanded the video clips tool in YouTube Studio to Shorts, so creators can turn segments of long-form videos, such as podcast episodes, into vertical Shorts. TeamYouTube announced the change Nov. 12 in a YouTube Help Community post aimed at podcast creators.

In an April post, YouTube said it would be “rolling out Video Clips to Shorts” later in the year. The tool’s help page has described the Shorts option since at least Sept. 10, according to an Internet Archive capture.

The help page explains that each Short created using this tool has the original video as its related video. A clickable link of the source video will be located underneath the channel handle. However, if the Short is either private, unlisted or Made for Kids, then the tool won’t add the link.

The automatic link gives teams with a video podcast or webinar series a way to send viewers from a Short back to the whole episode.

When they are in YouTube Studio, creators open one of their pre-existing long-form videos. On the left-hand side of the screen is a list of options including Clips and Shorts. If they choose Short, they have two ways to create the Short.

Here’s an example from YouTube’s demo showing the Short creation option in Studio.

They can enter timestamps into the timeline editor. Alternatively, they may select some text in their video’s transcript to create the Short.

Where available, the left-hand panel also shows AI-powered suggestions and an outline of the transcript that AI generates. YouTube’s post says it picks “moments in your episode that can be watched independently” so creators can review and edit them.

The selection screen places suggested Shorts beside the transcript and the original video.

The post describes how a widescreen episode becomes a Short:

“For Shorts, your 16:9 podcast episode will be transformed into a verticalized video, keeping the most important parts of the video in the frame.”

The help page says captions are added automatically where available, and creators can change their color, font, styling and position or turn them off.

Here’s the vertical preview from the same demo, with captions and a Create draft button.

For standard video clips, which the tool still makes, the help page’s tip is to add a link to the source video in the clip’s description. YouTube also recommends keeping only full episodes in podcast playlists.

YouTube’s help page for the clips tool lists AI-powered suggestions and outlines for creators in Brazil, Canada, France, Germany, India, Mexico, South Korea, Spain, the U.K. and the U.S. The supported languages are English, French, German, Hindi, Korean, Portuguese and Spanish.

In an April capture, the page limited suggestions to English-language videos in a podcast playlist in the U.S. and Canada. The current page doesn’t mention podcast playlists for suggestions.

The help page for the clips tool contains a statement which reads, “You won’t be penalized for using your content in a video clip or Short.” The statement’s link references YouTube’s channel monetization policies. The same language was found on Sept. 10 when the help page was captured. Earlier versions of the page also had the same response for use of a video clip under a question regarding penalties for copyright infringement.

On Oct. 1, YouTube said it would reduce the reach of channels that mainly aggregate or re-upload other creators’ clips without significant changes. In my coverage, I wrote that YouTube’s video on the change didn’t discuss creators cutting Shorts from their own long-form videos.

Unless it’s private, unlisted, or Made for Kids, a Short created with the clips tool will automatically link to its full-length episode. When you create a Short on another platform and upload it yourself to YouTube, YouTube’s related video help page has you add that link manually after uploading. To do this requires advanced feature access.

Beyond stating that creators won’t be penalized for using their own content in a clip or Short, the help page’s note didn’t address recommendations, which was specifically what the Oct. 1 Shorts changes were about.

According to YouTube’s post, they are currently working to expand the AI suggestion system to more of their creators in the near future but do not include specific countries, languages, nor time frames. If you have a YouTube channel located outside of the 10-country limit or the seven-language limit included on the help page, then this will be the list to reference prior to developing a clipping routine based on suggested content.

#7364. Higher Spend Came With Pricier Clicks For 14 B2B Google Advertisers via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Learn how to prioritize page fixes, check agent access and measure readiness for your 2027 SEO plan.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

One agency's data on 14 B2B advertisers finds the same non-brand Google Ads keywords cost more per click, even after controlling for their own spending.

To determine how much more the same non-branded Google Ads keywords cost 14 B2B advertisers per click during the months of April through September compared to the same time period last year, Grey Matter reviewed their Google Ads Search campaigns.

According to the agency report by Grey Matter issued in October, there was an increase of about 21% in cost-per-click (CPC) for the same keywords used by these 14 B2B advertisers, after accounting for changes in their spending, ad position and Quality Score. The report gives a likely range of 1% to 44% for that figure. It is based on client Google Ads Search campaign data only, so it does not include Performance Max, Shopping or Display campaigns. All 14 come from the agency’s client base, and its search team runs SEO and paid search programs for B2B companies.

Andy Hendricks, Grey Matter’s head of search, writes that he wanted to isolate what portion of the overall price change all advertisers experienced versus what portion was due to actions taken by each advertiser individually. Most available CPC benchmarks, such as published averages, do not account for this differentiation and simply provide an overall average CPC.

Grey Matter excluded broad match keywords since the searches they get matched to can change over time; keywords within Maximize Clicks campaigns also do not fit into the analysis. Brand and competitor keywords were also excluded from the overall estimate.

Grey Matter’s analysis is based on tracking 1,349 non-brand keywords, each within one account, for six quarters (April 2025 to September 2026). The year-over-year comparison covers April through September 2026 versus those months a year prior.

The headline estimate was derived from a regression model using data from all 14 advertisers in the report which controlled for campaign spend, impression share lost to budget, top-of-page position, and Quality Score. Every advertiser was given equal weight regardless of how much it spends.

With no controls applied, the same keywords cost 29% more year over year. Adding the two spend-related controls lowered that to 22%, and position and Quality Score took it to 21%.

A second estimation technique used in this analysis was a keyword price index. This measure covered 11 advertisers who had sufficient matching keywords from quarter to quarter.

The report’s regression ties every 10% increase in campaign spend to about 1.4% higher CPCs, on top of the market increase, or roughly 11% for doubled spend. The report doesn’t give a likely range for that figure.

The most extreme case in the sample nearly quadrupled its non-brand Search spend year over year, and its same-keyword CPCs rose 132%. The report says that advertiser’s brand keywords, on a flat budget, rose about 4% year over year in the third quarter.

The report sees a correlation between this pattern and automated bidding. It explains that a campaign using a strategy like Maximize Conversions spends a bigger budget by entering auctions it previously skipped, including higher positions, more competitive searches, and pricier times of day.

For the 11 advertisers covered by the keyword price index, the overall year-over-year increase in average non-brand CPC was 36%, including the advertisers’ own choices. Of that, 22 points were attributed to the market, and three additional points were attributed to increased spending. There was a three-point deduction due to various types of keyword, match type and bid strategy changes, leaving 14 points that the report’s first three parts don’t explain.

Grey Matter illustrates that breakdown in the chart below.

The majority of those 14 points occurred at a few of the advertisers who saw an approximate doubling of their CPCs, which the report said probably represented competition in their respective categories.

Actual CPC changes ranged from a 30% decrease to a 121% increase. Ten of the 11 paid more for the same keywords, and nine paid more overall. One advertiser kept its spending flat and saw the same keywords rise by 20%. After shifting some of its budget to pricier phrase-match keywords, its overall CPC increased by 40%.

With only 14 advertisers in this report, the likely range for the 21% estimate is wide. When the biggest spender is removed from the report’s data, the estimate drops to around 17%, and its likely range of -2% to +40% includes zero. When broad match keywords are included, the estimate rises to around 31%. According to Hendricks, compared with a year ago, that same click most likely costs about 20% more currently, and the true figure “could be meaningfully higher or lower”.

The report calls its findings correlation (and not proven causation). There are no references on the report page or in the PDF to the industries or markets of the advertisers used in the report, nor their currency.

The report doesn’t provide any conversion rate or cost per lead information; so, it can’t show whether these advertisers saw better or worse results along with higher CPCs. Jyll Saskin Gales argued in an April column for Search Engine Journal (SEJ) that under Smart Bidding, a high CPC is often a sign of a healthy account, and that accounts should be evaluated based on CPA or ROAS.

Alphabet’s third-quarter 10-Q reports that cost-per-click on Google Search & other properties rose 4% year over year in the quarter. Alphabet has defined this metric as click-driven revenue divided by total paid clicks and has cited several factors such as changes in the devices users access the internet on, geography, advertiser spending, and product mix as variables that affect it.

The metric is based on paid clicks through Google Search properties and other platforms owned by Alphabet, so it will track with the price of the clicks and also the mix of clicks being counted. Grey Matter’s estimate instead tracks the same keywords across 14 advertisers.

There is no direct confirmation or contradiction between the two figures since they measure entirely different things.

Grey Matter’s report holds the keyword and the account constant, while raw CPC averages also rise when advertisers buy different clicks. With 14 clients of one agency and a range from 1% to 44%, the report is a reference point for B2B non-brand Search, not a forecast for any single account.

You can run a same-keyword check on your own account by comparing the year-over-year change in what the same non-brand keywords cost with the change in the account’s average CPC. The report reads the gap between those two as the effect of keyword, match type and bid strategy changes. A same-keyword rise can still include the effect of higher spending.

Grey Matter’s data runs through September, so it doesn’t include fourth-quarter auctions. For 2027 budgets, the report’s guidance is to scale them gradually and assess each step by how much the additional clicks cost. For campaigns on automated bidding such as Maximize Conversions, the report also suggests deciding what the additional conversions are worth before raising the budget, and setting a target to match.

#7362. LinkedIn says influential voices are critical for AI chatbot citations

Let Social Media Today's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Artificial intelligence models are built to reward credibility, according to Robert Yanik, AI search marketing lead for LinkedIn Ads.

LinkedIn has shared some new notes on how brands can maximize their opportunities to be cited in artificial intelligence chatbot responses. The platform says that tapping into the credibility and authority of influencers can play a big part in seeing brands get referenced.

As explained by Robert Yanik, AI Search Marketing Lead for LinkedIn Ads: “AI models like ChatGPT, Gemini, and Copilot are built to reward credibility, and credibility, in their eyes, looks a lot like a real person with a demonstrated track record. LinkedIn ties every post to a verified identity, complete with employer history, skills, and a professional network.”

According to research cited by Yanik and conducted by Meltwater, AI chatbots are most likely to refer to LinkedIn posts generated by individual users who have more than 10,000 followers in the app.

As shown in this visual overview, accounts with over 10k followers and company pages make up 65% of LinkedIn citations in AI chatbot responses. Users with under 10k followers account for only 35% of these mentions.

“C-suite and VP-level articles earn higher citation rates per article than non-executive content, which shows this isn't about volume but about how AI models weigh authority," Yanik explained. "LinkedIn profile metadata, including title, company, and industry, acts as a direct authority signal that AI models use to judge credibility.”

Based on this, in order to maximize opportunities to be cited in AI responses, Yanik said that brands should look to activate a range of individuals in addition to their company page posts in order to spread their messaging.

In order to put this into practice, Yanik said brands should identify three or four themes to own, then encourage “credible employees” to post consistently about those themes. He added that brands should also identify one or two creators “whose audience overlaps with your buyers and explore a partnership.”

So, essentially, the advice here is to post a lot more on LinkedIn, though Yanik’s guidance extrapolates this recommendation with presumed insight into how AI models choose different sources.

But Yanik doesn’t outline how each AI tool decides on what source to cite, so this report is based on correlation, and really, LinkedIn is reliant on the established domain authority of its platform. And as such, this advice is less about helping businesses understand AI citations, and more about driving more LinkedIn activity.

Over the past few months, LinkedIn has been increasingly keen to highlight how AI chatbots commonly cite LinkedIn posts and articles in their generated replies.

As search behaviors evolve, this presents a significant opportunity, because while referral traffic from search engines has steadily declined for many websites, businesses that are reliant on those referrals are now looking for ways to tap alternate traffic drivers, like AEO, or Answer Engine Optimization, or GEO, Generative Engine Optimization.

As such, maybe putting more focus on LinkedIn could be the answer.

LinkedIn has tried to capitalize on this by sharing various guides and advice notes on what AI chatbots are seeking from web content, and how brands can rank higher in AI chatbot results.

The core of all of this guidance, however, is that brands need to post on LinkedIn, a lot, in order to piggyback on LinkedIn’s domain authority as a means to boost their presence. LinkedIn is totally reliant on its established trust and authority in this respect, but it’s not doing anything specific to better align with AI tools. Nonetheless, the company has seized upon the opportunity to present itself as an authoritative advisor on AI citations because of these stats.

These latest insights underline this by reinforcing that the only real way to optimize for AI chatbots based on LinkedIn posting is to post a heap to LinkedIn, and to share a lot of content that resonates with LinkedIn users, in the hopes that, eventually, this will help to boost brand and individual authority in the app. All of LinkedIn’s other AEO notes, such as its guides on how to structure articles for AI chatbots, are irrelevant if the creator doesn’t have an established presence.

In summary, posting on LinkedIn can have a positive impact on AEO or GEO efforts, and could see brands get more mentions in AI chatbot responses. But it’s not as simple as structuring content in a certain way. The true signal here is established trust and presence, which are the indicators that AI chatbots can use to measure relevance and expertise. Platforms including LinkedIn and Reddit can provide such through user engagement data, including how many likes a post has, how many upvotes, and how many people follow the creator. Those signals can be integrated into a chatbot's code on how to identify what's likely to be an authoritative response to a query.

#7359. ChatGPT Ads Vs. Google Search Ads: What Early Data Shows via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Learn how to prioritize page fixes, check agent access and measure readiness for your 2027 SEO plan.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

ChatGPT competes for ad budgets, but revenue and survey data don't show how much left search. Its ad visits earned less in early data; per dollar is estimated.

This past week, OpenAI rolled out cost-target bidding in its ChatGPT Ads platform. These ads first launched in February. By Aug. 31, OpenAI reported that its ads product had achieved a $1 billion annualized run rate.

The question for a paid search team is whether ChatGPT Ads now compete with Google Search Ads for advertising dollars, and whether the early results make a case for moving some of their budget to ChatGPT Ads.

ChatGPT Ads are competing for advertisers’ budgets. However, the revenue metrics, survey data, and Google’s disclosures I reviewed give no indication of how many dollars have been diverted away from Google Search Ads to ChatGPT Ads.

In terms of performance metrics, a ChatGPT ad click visit to an advertiser’s website generated about one-third the revenue of a paid search visit in U.S. traffic. The working paper in my Nov. 2 coverage provided the pooled revenue metric from 102 different websites with transaction and revenue tracking. The authors of that paper called the comparison descriptive. One of the authors’ universities also released a press statement saying that for every dollar an advertiser spends on ChatGPT Ads, they receive $0.52 back in revenue (compared to $3.40 for every dollar spent on Google Search).

Only the first of these performance metrics was actually measured. The second figure combined that measured performance metric with the average cost-per-click pricing from one ChatGPT account and the median cost-per-click pricing from 16 paid search accounts. So the second figure varies with what an advertiser pays per click on each of those channels.

An Oct. 21 engineering post from OpenAI puts the run rate above $1 billion, with more than 100,000 advertisers having run ads since launch. Alphabet reported $63.35 billion in Search & other revenue for the third quarter, up 12%, which I covered on Oct. 28.

On Alphabet’s earnings call, Chief Business Officer Philipp Schindler cited “the lower monetization we were seeing in AI Mode” as one factor in slower Search growth, without giving a figure or a baseline.

Google Search was the most-mentioned potential source of future ChatGPT ad spending in a Bank of America survey of 117 advertisers and agencies, TheStreet reported on Oct. 14. I haven’t seen the bank’s note, and the survey asked about plans. It doesn’t measure money that has moved.

Neither company’s revenue figures show spending that shifted from one platform to the other. Google can continue to grow even as some advertisers move part of their budgets elsewhere.

In U.S. traffic, paid search generated $2.45 in revenue per session versus $0.89 with ChatGPT ad sessions, and transactions per session came to 1.17% against 0.37%.

These pooled figures are based on a total of 60 weeks (through Aug. 22). For U.S. traffic, there were up to 28 weeks of ChatGPT ad sessions available for analysis.

Kaiser and Schulze built a statistical model that controlled for website, device type, and week, and found that ChatGPT ad sessions generated lower rates of conversion and lower revenue per session than paid search. These results were robust even after removing individual advertisers. Out of 87 different advertisers who used both paid search and ChatGPT ads in U.S. traffic, 84 produced less revenue per session using ChatGPT ads.

About 20% of the 102 websites studied were services, software, education, or finance websites. A table footnote explains that these websites report online conversions as bookings, leads, licenses, or sign-ups. However, the appendix calls a transaction a completed order, and it does not explain how the authors defined the relationship between these two types of metrics.

Because advertisers chose to buy ChatGPT ads, the authors do not argue causally that ChatGPT is worse for advertisers than paid search. Rather, the authors view their work as “descriptive rather than causal,” a record of what these websites received from each advertising channel. They do not provide any evidence as to whether switching advertising dollars from paid search to ChatGPT under matched campaigns would be beneficial or detrimental to specific advertisers.

Data for this study were donated by Grips Intelligence, where Kaiser, a former employee, works as a scientific advisor. The authors disclose that Kaiser has received a salary and virtual company shares from Grips Intelligence. The authors add that Grips Intelligence played no role in conducting the research described in this study.

I contacted OpenAI about this study and received an email response from an OpenAI spokesperson saying the study “captures an earlier period of ChatGPT Ads’ development and extrapolates platform performance from a small sample.” The statement also says OpenAI has kept launching products since the ads launched, with Target CPA and Target CPC among the recent ones.

Kaiser and Schulze express the difference in terms of value for money, revenue per session divided by click price. Treating each session as a paid click, that works out to about $0.52 per ad dollar for ChatGPT ($0.89 ÷ $1.72) and $3.40 for search ($2.45 ÷ $0.72), the two figures in the Frankfurt School release. The authors report that an ad visit from ChatGPT returns about one-third the value of a search visit at about 2.4 times the price. In their abstract, they state that ChatGPT ads are worth less than a sixth the value for money of Google Search. A table provides a reported ChatGPT price of 6.57 times what would be necessary to produce the same return on investment as Search.

The Frankfurt School release cites the difference as “6.6 times better value for money” for Google and reports this in revenue per ad dollar. Its $0.52 and $3.40 figures are not provided within the body of the paper, and the release itself gives no explanation of how they were calculated.

There is no single price given for search, but rather a median price of $0.72, which comes from 16 mostly U.S. accounts that have linked Google Ads cost records. This price was not matched to the revenue each site generated.

The ChatGPT price is $1.72, which Opascope, an agency that runs paid media for clients, published in June. It covers one account and about $60,000 in spend over 15 days, on campaigns the agency scaled after they showed promise. Opascope wrote that the numbers aren’t an average of every test it launched, and it reported a 1.49x ROAS.

Just one website in the sample did as well as Opascope’s account on both conversion rate and implied revenue per visit, and it had only nine ChatGPT ad sessions.

The authors suggest that $1.72 is a conservative price as it is toward the low end of what practitioners report and below the $3-$5 OpenAI suggests be used as a starting maximum bid. Also, bidding is based on a maximum value, which is simply the cap an advertiser sets, not necessarily what each click will cost.

Resampling advertisers, while keeping the search price constant, gives the authors a range of $0.05 to $0.57 around a ChatGPT click price of about $0.26 that would match search’s return. Neither the ChatGPT click price nor the search price was changed.

Advertising agencies have provided reports about their individual ChatGPT Ads campaign data for one advertiser, and separately, Brooke Osmundson reported in September that advertisers lacked the context to assess what they were being told by the numbers in such reporting.

StubGroup provides ChatGPT ads management services. It ran Google ad campaigns and ChatGPT Ads campaigns for an advertiser who is a B2B compliance company, for 30 days. The clicks on ChatGPT ads cost $0.76 a click. The clicks on Google search campaigns cost $10.46 a click. The cost per lead with ChatGPT was $92.21; with Google it was $157.57. The conversion rates were 0.8% with ChatGPT, and 6.64% with Google.

The agency said that while the advertising campaigns run through Google have years of optimization behind them, the leads generated through those campaigns appear to be converting to customers at a greater rate so far than the leads generated through ChatGPT ads. As a result of this, the cost per customer appears to be lower for Google. How many customers will be ultimately generated from the leads created through both platforms, StubGroup does not yet know, due to the client’s long sales cycle. Additionally, the agency said that the $0.76 per click was low when compared to the CPCs of some of its other clients.

Workshop Digital used its own funds to pay for $2,319 worth of clicks on ChatGPT Ads. Workshop Digital’s CPC for ChatGPT ads was $3.14. For Google Search the CPC was $6.76. The CTR for Workshop Digital’s ChatGPT ads was 1.1%. For Google, the CTR was 5.2%.

Workshop Digital received zero qualified leads from its ChatGPT ads, and two qualified leads from Google. The agency called the two types of advertising campaigns “not a direct comparison,” although it didn’t provide the spend amount or dates for Google Search as part of the comparison.

Brafton, which sells ChatGPT Ads services, ran one click campaign with one ad from June 17 to Aug. 28. It spent $1,524.11 for 263 clicks and one form submission. Its Google and LinkedIn comparisons are outside benchmarks, and its director of paid media, Zach Shah, said ChatGPT ads are “priced like search, but seem to behave like paid social.”

All three agency reports came down to what happened after the click, the stage with the fewest results.

Additionally, OpenAI pointed me to some customer success stories via its ad page. One such example is from Jotform. According to this example, their cost per signup is approximately 30% less than with paid search. This figure is based on Jotform’s internal data collected during July; however, no costs are provided behind it, nor does it detail how the sign-ups were attributed. Sign-ups do not equal sales.

A paid search team may be asked to shift some portion of search spend to ChatGPT on the strength of a per-dollar figure based upon another party’s click costs. For an account paying a number of dollars per click on search, a number greater than the median of the paper’s data, there are differing starting points for making such comparisons. The basis for comparison that makes sense begins with actual spend and attributed revenue on each channel for the same period of time and using the same attribution settings.

The size of the test will affect how much an ecommerce account testing ChatGPT Ads can learn from a weak result. In all, 18 of the paper’s 102 sites recorded transactions resulting from ChatGPT ads; a single zero does not distinguish a weak channel from a small test. So when reporting to stakeholders, the number of sessions associated with each ad campaign belongs beside the revenue figures, because what a zero means is quite different depending on whether there were a few dozen or a few thousand visits.

To compare results, lead-gen accounts also need to define a qualified lead in exactly the same terms on both channels and give those leads sufficient opportunity to convert. This is why StubGroup’s customer comparison is still open, since its lower-cost leads have not yet turned into lower-cost customers. While a cost per signup was used by Jotform as the metric in their case study, it is further removed from revenue than a cost per customer.

A team planning to present Ads Manager results either against the paper’s data or against its Google Ads performance metrics will first need to document its attribution settings. On OpenAI’s Measure Results page, Ads Manager conversion totals may include eligible view-through conversions, and advertisers select from a 7-day, 14-day, or 30-day click window. The view-through window can be set to one day or disabled, and both windows employ last-touch logic. The paper tracked visits that were attributable to the ad.

Ads Manager tracks clicks, while analytics uses sessions. There can be differences between these two numbers. On OpenAI’s basics page, the company says ad-review crawling programs may access landing pages that contain tracking parameters, and analytics tools may report these visits as paid traffic even if they do not represent billable clicks. The Measure Results page provides additional explanations for these discrepancies, including redirects, consent settings, and browser-based blocking. Only by assuming clicks and sessions align can the release’s $0.52 be calculated from reported revenue per session and cost per click.

OpenAI’s ads FAQ says that they do not currently provide performance benchmarks. In addition, all four OpenAI help pages I reviewed provided a suggested starting bid, but none provided any information about click prices across advertisers. Neither did OpenAI’s statement. One advertiser’s cost is documented in the research paper. Each agency CPC was unique to its specific case.

There were no monthly or bid-type breakdowns within the research paper, so it could not establish if the outcomes had been affected by OpenAI adding conversion-optimized campaigns in June. Data from the research paper ceased prior to when OpenAI implemented bid adjustments, which went into effect this month.

On Oct. 28, Google published an ads post stating that CTR for sponsored and organic product listings in AI Mode were similar. This was based on two weeks of internal U.S. data. Google gave no absolute rates. That was the single performance figure for AI Mode ads in my review of Google documentation.

Two of the bid options now offered for new ChatGPT Ads campaigns didn’t exist during the paper’s data window. Since Nov. 9, new conversion campaigns that want a cost target use Target CPA, and new click campaigns can use Target CPC, as I covered this week. The option with no cost target is Intelligent bidding, previously named Maximize Results, according to OpenAI’s bidding page. Beginning with Nov. 18, all values submitted using the legacy API bid fields are treated as the average-cost target when creating a new campaign or changing an ad group’s bid type. Max Bid and Max CPC will officially retire for existing campaigns on Jan. 1, 2027.

Performance improvement is not shown in the new options. Noting which objective, bid strategy, and attribution windows produced each fourth-quarter result will help a team decide if the metrics generated this year have enough similarities to compare next year.

Follow on YouTube. Matt G. Southern is the Senior News Writer at Search Engine Journal, where he’s covered Google, SEO, ...

#7358. How AI and social media are changing the media mix

Think about the last time you made a purchase online. Where did you start? A social post may have sparked your interest, while search or an AI tool helped you decide what to do next. This kind of cross-channel shopping is becoming more common. According to the 2027 Global Digital Report, 42% of online adults use social media to research purchases, and 30.4% of internet users discover new brands through social media. AI also plays a clear role: 26% use it for brand research, and 17% discover brands through LLM platforms such as ChatGPT.

Generative AI has moved quickly from novelty to a practical shopping aid. In a short time, personalized recommendations and shopping assistants have become familiar parts of online shopping. New applications keep appearing, giving consumers more ways to find useful information before they buy.

Online adults now use an average of 3.8 channels when researching brands, up from 3.65 two years ago. People are using those channels at different points in the purchase process.

One in three people globally already use generative AI each month, and usage is likely to keep growing. Social media is also claiming more of people's daily attention. Globally, people spend an average of 42 minutes more on social media than they did just a year ago. Social commerce is expanding too. More than one in four people now make purchases through social platforms, generating an estimated $586 billion annually.

For marketers, this is an energizing moment. More choice gives brands more ways to meet audiences and support their decisions. A strong media mix starts with clear goals and a close reading of digital behavior. Intelligence is always in style, and that understanding should guide where brands invest.

Where and how do our audiences spend time online?

Most leading social platforms share large portions of their audiences. For example, 78.5% of people who use YouTube also use Instagram.

Strong media mixes prioritize the platforms where audiences are most engaged and where each channel can play a distinct role.

The differences between platforms can guide those decisions. Instagram is the favorite social platform for 17.8% of online adults. TikTok has the highest average daily time per user, at one hour and 55 minutes. WhatsApp leads in average daily sessions, at 20.7.

Local data makes the picture even more useful. X has the highest daily usage time in Japan, at 32 minutes and 44 seconds. Threads has the highest rate of monthly usage in Taiwan, at 40.8%. Pinterest has the highest advertising reach in the Netherlands, at 56.7%. In Ireland, Reddit leads in average daily sessions per user, at 7.88.

These details turn broad platform trends into practical investment decisions.

Which social media platforms influence our AI visibility most?

LLMs can draw on social media content when responding to prompts. Tracking AI visibility over time can show which channels influence the responses most relevant to your audiences.

Each brand will see a different pattern. Reddit may matter most for product-comparison questions. Creator content on TikTok may shape answers about trends or recommendations. For a B2B company, LinkedIn may matter most when AI platforms respond to questions about its expertise and offerings.

These insights give marketers evidence about how audiences find information and which platforms deserve closer attention.

The modern customer journey is more connected than ever, and that makes measurement more important. Last-touch attribution captures only the final step, so it can miss earlier influence.

Multichannel measurement gives marketers a fuller view of how channels contribute along the path to purchase. Bringing those signals into one view helps teams see which interactions support results and where channels reinforce one another.

Digital behavior data shows that AI is changing the role social media plays in discovery. As consumers move from one digital touchpoint to another, marketers can build a clearer view of how those interactions connect.

Download the 2027 Global Digital Report to explore the latest data on where and how audiences spend time online. Then, learn how Meltwater can help you apply that context to the audiences and channels that matter most.

#7355. When The WordPress Plugin Update Is The Security Risk: Why SEOs Should Pay Attention via @sejournal, @cshel

Identify practical ways to improve location visibility in AI-generated answers and recommendations.

This rundown shows how to test briefs, audits, or rank reporting with a four-layer system built on your own data.

Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Learn how to prioritize page fixes, check agent access and measure readiness for your 2027 SEO plan.

Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Assess SEO risks from compromised WordPress plugin updates with deployment records and file checks that reveal what version numbers alone cannot.

I’ve worked with WordPress for more than 20 years, and for most of that time, keeping plugins updated has been among the first recommendations I’ve made when discussing website security. It’s still good advice. Known vulnerabilities don’t become less dangerous because updating a site is inconvenient. The advice does carry an assumption that most of us rarely bother to examine: The update we’re installing is the software its developer actually intended to release.

For SEOs, this is more than an abstract concern about application security. When someone gets unauthorized access to a website, they may change the pages search engines see, introduce spam, tamper with redirects or indexing directives, or alter content without the editorial team’s knowledge. We can spend days investigating those symptoms as routine technical SEO problems while completely missing the reason they’re happening.

There have now been several recent WordPress incidents in which the trusted delivery mechanism was part of the problem, so let’s look at them together. Examining them as a group is more illuminating than treating any single developer’s bad week as the big story.

In June 2024, malicious updates appeared in several WordPress.org-hosted plugins, including Social Warfare. Wordfence’s investigation traced the compromise to developer accounts that used previously exposed credentials. The injected code could create administrator accounts and exfiltrate information, and researchers also found code designed to inject SEO spam. Roughly 35,000 installations were potentially exposed, although Wordfence couldn’t say how many had actually received the malicious updates. This is an especially useful example for our profession because the search-related damage wasn’t hypothetical: SEO spam was part of the attacker’s toolkit.

The following July, Gravity Forms disclosed that some copies of versions 2.9.11.1 and 2.9.12 available for manual download had been modified maliciously. According to Gravity Forms’ incident notice, the exposure was confined to particular manual downloads and Composer installations during July 9-10, 2025. Its separate automatic-update service was not compromised. That distinction is important. Two organizations could have been using ostensibly the same software while facing different risks because they obtained it through different channels.

The more recent incident involved Rank Math SEO PRO. Rank Math’s incident disclosure says a subset of version 3.0.123 packages downloaded or updated from October 15 at 20:02 UTC through October 16 at 07:12 UTC contained malware capable of compromising sites if activated. Rank Math removed the affected files, provided a clean 3.0.124 release, and advised affected customers to treat their installations as compromised. Wordfence’s advisory classified the issue as critical, with a CVSS score of 9.8. The free Rank Math plugin distributed through WordPress.org was not affected.

The three cases aren’t identical. One involved compromised developer accounts and poisoned repository updates, another concerned certain official manual downloads, and the third affected specific packages of a commercial SEO plugin. They do, however, share a problem that ordinary update advice doesn’t adequately address: the site owner could get into trouble while obtaining software from a source they had good reason to trust.

The Rank Math release timeline makes this unusually clear. According to the Rank Math changelog, version 3.0.123 appeared on Oct. 7, but the affected distribution window didn’t begin until Oct. 15. A site reporting that version number might have received the original clean files or a compromised package, depending on when it was updated. The installed version alone cannot resolve the question.

Imagine managing a hundred client sites, or an enterprise portfolio with different brands, teams, and hosting providers. A security advisory comes out and somebody asks which properties are exposed. You can generate a tidy plugin inventory in minutes, but a spreadsheet of version numbers may not tell you what was actually installed. You need deployment timestamps, a record of the package source and, where possible, a way to compare the installed files with verified clean artifacts. Gravity Forms presented a related challenge because the installation method determined whether a particular download was implicated.

I don’t expect SEO practitioners to start auditing build pipelines. I do expect us to understand the limits of the information we routinely use to assess the health of a site. A dashboard that says a plugin is current doesn’t certify that the underlying files are trustworthy, and installing a clean update doesn’t necessarily remove accounts, backdoors, or stolen credentials left behind during a compromise.

See also: From Plugins To Performance: What CMOs Need To Ask About Their WordPress Stack

In an enterprise environment, ownership of the website is often split across several groups. Marketing may choose the SEO software, development handles deployments, a hosting provider manages updates, and security gets called when someone notices suspicious activity. The SEO team is nevertheless expected to account for what the search engines can crawl, index, and understand. Those responsibilities overlap whether the organizational chart acknowledges it or not.

Consider how a compromise might first appear in our regular work. Search Console begins reporting unfamiliar URLs. A crawl finds redirects that weren’t present last week. Canonical tags have changed without a corresponding release, or the rendered page no longer matches what the content team published. Depending on the attacker’s access, visitors, and crawlers might even be served different content. None of those observations proves a security breach, and I am not suggesting they were documented outcomes of the Rank Math incident. They are reasons to include compromise in the set of explanations we consider instead of assuming every unexpected technical change is a configuration error.

That can be particularly consequential when an SEO has spotted a symptom but doesn’t know whom to tell. We don’t need to become the malware response team. We do need an established route for escalating suspicious changes, preferably one worked out before an incident has everyone comparing logs and asking who owns what. The 2024 SEO-spam injection case demonstrates why that coordination belongs in an SEO conversation rather than being filed away as somebody else’s security problem.

See also: Who Owns Web Performance? Building A Framework For Digital Accountability

WordPress itself has been looking at the trust problem. In June 2026, Matt Mullenweg announced Protect the Shire, initially describing a cooldown for new plugin and theme releases before they reached sites through WordPress.org’s automatic-update infrastructure. WordPress has also documented an automated security review process intended to identify potentially risky changes. The appeal is straightforward: a period of scrutiny may catch a compromised release before it is distributed widely, although delaying a genuine emergency fix creates a competing risk.

That protection has boundaries. Commercial plugins delivered by their vendors’ own update systems don’t necessarily pass through the same WordPress.org review process, even though all the plugins appear in the same administrative interface. The FAIR project, operating under Linux Foundation governance, is taking a different approach by developing federated and independent software repositories. Protect the Shire focuses on checks within one major distribution route, while FAIR focuses on diversifying and verifying the routes themselves. Neither approach should be mistaken for a guarantee that every package reaching a website is safe.

For people responsible for websites, the practical question is less about choosing a philosophical side in the distribution debate and more about knowing where each update originates and what checks apply along the way.

An SEO team doesn’t need to turn every plugin upgrade into a full security review. It would be useful, though, to know whether the organization retains update dates and deployment records, whether its commercial plugins use separate delivery systems, and who can verify file integrity when a vendor identifies an affected download window. It is equally worth knowing whether the recovery plan extends beyond restoring yesterday’s backup to checking for unauthorized accounts, persistence mechanisms, and exposed credentials.

I would also ask who receives reports of suspicious URLs, unexpected redirects, or changes to indexing instructions. We already know how to inspect rendered pages, compare crawls, review logs, and investigate search performance anomalies. Those skills make us quite capable of noticing that a website isn’t behaving as it should. They don’t automatically tell us why, which is where an agreed escalation process becomes useful.

None of this is a reason to stop updating plugins, or an excuse to infer that a particular vendor is incompetent because it experienced an incident. The root cause of a compromise cannot responsibly be guessed from the fact that malware turned up in a package. What these examples do show is that familiar update channels and reassuring version numbers aren’t the whole story. We spend a great deal of time checking what a website actually serves rather than trusting what a configuration screen says it should serve. It seems reasonable to apply some of that same curiosity to the software we’re installing on it.

#7350. Media Briefing: The IAB finds AI measurement standards elusive for publishers amid platform data gaps

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This Media Briefing covers the latest in media trends for Digiday+ members and is distributed over email every Thursday at 10 a.m. ET. More from the series →

This week’s Media Briefing looks at how the IAB’s months-long effort to standardize AI attribution ran into a roadblock: the lack of visibility into what happens inside AI platforms.

The Interactive Advertising Bureau spent months trying to determine how the industry should attribute and credit conversions influenced by AI. But it ran into a problem: you can’t standardize what you can’t see.

Digiday first reported in August that the IAB was developing an AI attribution framework to standardize measuring and crediting AI’s role in conversions, and the signals to quantify AI’s influence on purchase decisions. The IAB’s working group included tech companies, publishers, agencies, measurement vendors and brands — including People Inc., Walmart, WPP Media, Emberos, Criteo, Bluefish, Microsoft, Brandlight, Tinuiti and Prudential Financial. The resulting “AI-Era Attribution Blueprint,” was published today.

The blueprint addresses some of the questions behind the effort, but it also underscores how much remains unmeasurable. Recommendations, consumer decisions and even transactions may happen inside AI platforms, but that data isn’t yet accessible to advertisers or measurement providers.

“Going in, I thought that we were going to help figure out how credit was assigned through the process. I realized actually what this document was starting to become was more of a call to action to the industry,” Caroline Giegerich, the IAB’s vp of AI said during a virtual IAB roundtable on Monday. “There are evidence layers that are missing. We’ve started to have conversations on that, but there’s still room to go. But without remarking on what’s missing, what we want to see, and AI’s influence in the purchase funnel, I don’t think we’re going to get there. So it kind of started in one place, and over the course of four months, ended in another.”

As Digiday previously reported, the measurement gap has prompted a growing number of startups to pursue solutions.

The IAB’s blueprint offers a framework for understanding AI’s role in driving business outcomes, while acknowledging that data gaps make its influence difficult to reliably measure and attribute. It also underscores a broader challenge for the industry: as publishers expand their GEO offerings to brands, measurement remains a major obstacle.

But publishers are experimenting with ways to address parts of the measurement gap. Time is working with Mobian to measure the performance of the ads it serves to AI agents, for example, and Bustle Digital Group is working with Profound to measure its GEO campaign performance for brands. Those efforts offer visibility into specific activity, but they don’t yet solve the broader challenges of tying AI’s influence to sales.

AI search makes it more difficult for brands to measure how LLMs influence consumer purchases, because impressions, clicks and referral data aren’t always available when an AI assistant guides consumers from product discovery to purchase decisions. And even when an AI recommendation does lead to a sale, those conversions can be incorrectly attributed to search, direct traffic, or other channels, according to the IAB’s report.

That attribution gap can distort marketing investment decisions, with spend going to channels that capture conversions rather than those that influenced them. Similarweb data cited in IAB’s report found consumers who received a brand recommendation from an AI assistant are 2.5 times more likely to visit that brand’s website within seven days, but only 8.8% of those visits come directly from AI referrals. Instead, 55.9% are attributed to search and 19.9% to direct traffic.

The IAB framework focuses on three areas. It classifies who or what created the intent, made the decision, and executed the transaction. It defines additional claims AI involvement creates, and the evidence each one requires. And it highlights the transparency and measurement gaps that make those claims difficult to verify. (More detail is available in the framework).

The clearest case of this gap is when publisher content is behind an AI recommendation, the IAB noted in its report. If a consumer asks an AI answer engine for the best option in a category, and the answer draws on a publisher’s review, the consumer may not click the citation (a link to the publisher’s review), and it gets reported as a search or direct sale by the retailer.

“The review shaped the sale, and the publisher is eligible for a contributory claim. The evidence differs depending on how publisher content informed the answer. Content used in model training is generally not traceable from outside the model. Grounding and citation are more observable: publishers may be able to see when their content is cited or reflected in an AI response, although citation alone does not establish how much that content influenced the answer. What remains largely missing is the connection between those sourcing signals and downstream conversions. Until that link exists, the contributory claim cannot be fully substantiated,” the IAB report reads.

The IAB’s recommendations span AI platforms, brands, agencies and publishers. Platforms should disclose whether recommendations are paid or organic, which sources inform them and label AI-originated traffic consistently.

Brands and agencies should apply the framework’s claim-and-evidence standards across platforms, while publishers should identify claims about their content’s influence and the evidence needed to support them, per the report.

“Publishers are feeding the answers but they don’t have the receipts,” said Jaime Schultheis, head of global data partnerships at Bombora, where she leads a network of B2B publishers, brand sites and data providers.

The IAB framework does accomplish defining the parts of the AI measurement journey, which can help publishers compare these between vendors and their different measurement structures, Jon Roberts, People Inc.’s chief innovation officer, said during the roundtable.

“Everyone’s pitching you on their custom proprietary measurement solution, and they’re all using the same terms, and the terms all mean something different because they’ve all designed these in a silo and are racing to get your business, and they’re all unique,” he said.

There are some signs that the measurement gap may narrow soon. The U.K.’s Competition and Markets Authority (CMA) is requiring Google to provide U.K. publishers with clickthrough data for its AI search features by Dec. 3. OpenAI is also testing an analytics dashboard that counts how often a business shows up in ChatGPT’s answers and how many clicks those appearances send to its site.

“We’re waiting [with] bated breath,” said Graham Howes, svp of distribution and AI intelligence at BDG, adding that this data from OpenAI and Google would be incredibly valuable for its GEO offering.

During the roundtable, Roberts noted that without this data, publishers are effectively “flying blind.” Sharing analytics benefits both publishers and AI platforms by helping them identify business opportunities, improve products and better serve their users, he said. Third-party measurement would still be important for independently verifying platforms’ own data, Roberts added.

Brands want this measurement gap solved just as much as publishers. Ross Bucholc, associate media director at Prudential, said in the roundtable that the lack of first-party data from AI platforms is holding back his ability to unlock more ad budgets going to LLMs.

Even with better visibility into AI activity, connecting that activity to sales remains difficult. Josh Blyska, head of insights at Profound, told Digiday that existing attribution tools struggle to connect AI search’s influence on consumer purchase decisions to conversions. Platforms like Profound can measure AI visibility, citations and how brands appear in AI answers, but can’t reliably track whether those interactions lead to purchases.

In a recent analysis of browser-session data, Profound found one in three consumers had a relevant AI conversation about their purchase within a month that went unattributed, Blyska said. (The dataset didn’t include Google’s AI Mode or AI Overviews.)

“It’s a really tricky problem right now,” he said.

Ultimately, the challenge is not just measuring a brand’s visibility within AI platforms but proving that visibility drives business results. John Sills, head of sales, North America at The Guardian, said the industry needs a more consistent way to understand how AI patterns surface and answer questions.

“We’re not measuring CTRs here. There’s no way of really understanding where the attribution is coming from,” he said. “These LLMs are in the ad business now… In the same way you can call your account manager at Facebook and try to optimize a social campaign or on YouTube, we need that level of account management to help guide the best practices.”

“We are capable, and AI companies are capable of reaching deals that give us fair compensation for content and referring to our site. Ninety-five percent of the AI referrals we get now come through OpenAI. So to me, that’s a win for them and a win for us if that’s how people want to access the news. So when people say deals like that aren’t possible or we don’t deserve compensation, I don’t really buy it.”

— Matt Murray, Washington Post executive editor, speaking about its deal with OpenAI, at Web Summit in Lisbon, Portugal.

$330 million: The reported price under consideration for The New York Times Company’s potential acquisition of Letterboxd, an online community for film enthusiasts.

2.7 billion: The number of times Ziff Davis’ content was cited in Google’s AI Overviews and AI Mode in Q3, nearly 10 times the clicks from Google Search those sites received.

37%: Ziff Davis’ YOY decline in Google referrals.

44%: BuzzFeed’s total YOY revenue decline in Q3 2026.

#7345. Yahoo becomes first licensee of ‘Spotify-like’ collective AI content licensing model

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

AI content licensing has largely comprised of one-off deals between publishers and tech companies. RSL, which is being renamed The Internet Licensing Collective (ILC), is pursuing a collective model instead, with Yahoo as its first licensee.

The deal means publishers of all sizes can license their content for use in Yahoo’s AI-powered search engine Scout and get paid based on how that content is used.

Digiday spoke with ILC co-founder Doug Leeds about how this model works and how publishers can earn revenue.

A licensee like Yahoo pays a blanket licensing fee for access to content in the collective. Leeds declined to say how much Yahoo was paying, but the fee is tied to the “demand-side revenue.”  Yahoo declined to be interviewed for this story.

As a licensee’s business scales, the amount available for publisher compensation can grow as well.

“It allows businesses that are making a lot of money off of the content to keep that content in place and sustainable. But it also allows for entrants,” Leeds said. “As the revenue scales, the percentage scales. So more money comes out. But that also means that entrants can come in, and there can be new use cases and new players and new products, and all of that’s important to the evolution of what we’re doing to the market.”

A new report out today from publishing analytics firm Mather Economics estimated that collective licensing for AI could generate publisher compensation equal to 15% to 35% of AI company revenue, a potential market that in three years could surpass the U.S. display advertising market. The analysis was based on the current annual revenue from the two biggest frontier models, OpenAI and Anthropic, and an economic model created by Mather Economics incorporating AI training-data scaling laws.

Leeds likened the licensing collective to a Spotify-like model.

“It’s kind of like Spotify, but also a song is a song is a song, and content is very different. And so we had to come up with these rules that made sense specifically for digital content,” Leeds said.

Leeds said ILC worked closely with its publisher collective to figure out those rules. The first step was to define a “segmented royalty pool.” This separates licensing revenue by AI company, rather than combining payments into a single fund shared by all participating publishers. That means publishers only receive a share of the revenue generated by AI products that actually use their content.

More AI and tech companies are testing pay-per-value licensing models. Digiday has previously reported that Google and Microsoft are testing and developing these models, which compensate publishers when their content meaningfully contributes to AI-generated responses, rather than paying based on how frequently their content is used. It’s a shift from flat-fee licensing deals to more dynamic pricing, based on the value publishers’ content delivers to AI companies.

When an AI company signs a licensing agreement with ILC, that money goes into its own royalty pool. Rather than combining all these payments into one enormous fund and distributing them across participating publishers, ILC keeps the royalty pools separate.

This protects specialized publishers from having their revenue diluted, Leeds explained. A general-purpose AI search engine like Yahoo Scout might use large quantities of content from hundreds or thousands of publishers, but a specialized healthcare AI product might use a much smaller amount of content from a handful of medical-focused publishers, and that specialized content could be considerably more valuable to the healthcare company, he said.

ILC wanted to ensure that a publisher’s compensation reflects the value of the AI product using its content, not simply how much content it contributes to the overall licensing ecosystem.

“An AI vertical in health might be very low volume, but much higher specific value. So if your content gets used in that, you should split that high value, not get diluted by all the content that got used by Yahoo,” Leeds said.

Over 3,000 publishers are now part of the ILC, he noted.

The payments are determined by what Leeds called “allocation rules.” These take into account how much content is used, like grounding, retrieval or displayed in an AI response, and whether the publisher is credited or cited as a source.

For example, an AI product that uses a publisher’s content to check a fact might receive a lower allocation than one that displays detailed election results and explicitly credits the Associated Press, Leeds said. He did not disclose the exact formula used to calculate each publisher’s share.

The allocation rules are intended to discourage AI companies from choosing content simply because it’s cheaper, and instead incentivize them to use what they consider the highest-quality sources, he said.

Publishers can also decide who they want to license to. Right now, their only option is Yahoo. But as more licensees sign up, they can go into their ILC portal and select which AI companies they do and don’t want to license their content to. For example, if a publisher signs a separate content licensing deal with an AI company, they can take their content out of the collective. In the portal, they can also see what their content is being used for, such as RAG or training.

RSL, which stood for the Really Simple Licensing (RSL), was two things: a group of publishers working on a collective licensing agreement, and the organization behind the RSL Protocol, a standard that publishers can use in robots.txt to describe in machine-readable language what their terms of service are. Publishers can ask AI companies for a pay-per-crawl or pay-per-inference fee through the RSL Protocol, for example. “Thousands” are currently using this standard, Leeds said.

But Leeds wanted a new name for the non-profit organization that reflected the publisher collective, to make it clear that this is a licensing model that any publisher can join, he said.

Leeds said ILC is currently in talks with “some” of the major AI companies interested in the licensing collective. He declined to name which ones.

“You can do a deal with a one-off, and that’s fantastic. But that doesn’t invest in the infrastructure of content in general,” he said.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

Bustle Digital Group has teamed up with Profound to help brands like Pampers and Innisfree boost their visibility in AI recommendations in a new GEO-offering.

Twitch is swinging big with a new short-form content push that should help more creators get discovered on-platform.

#7342. Why user-first ad experiences build the strongest app businesses

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Today’s mobile users are searching, streaming, scrolling and shopping in bursts as they go about their day. According to RealityMine, the average smartphone user interacts with around 18 different apps per day, racking up to 200 app sessions daily.¹ In each of those moments, attention is won second by second — and lost just as quickly.

Capturing value in those seconds is remarkably difficult. When audience acquisition is expensive, and engagement is just as hard to earn, app studios cannot afford ad experiences that cause churn.

In-app ads are a core part of the overall app experience, yet monetization and user retention are still too often treated as competing priorities. The assumption is that earning more revenue requires heavier ad loads, which damages the user experience, while protecting that experience means leaving money on the table.

“Disruptive ads have a severe, compounding negative impact on both current engagement and future user acquisition,” said Scott Sheffer, vp of global partnerships on Google’s sell-side monetization team. “Disruptive ads not only drive current users away but also prompt negative reviews, discouraging future users from downloading the app. Seems intuitive, but now there is data to back that.”

According to a Deloitte and Google AdMob study, a single encounter with a disruptive ad increases a user’s likelihood to churn by 6% to 7%.² When those experiences repeat, 52% of gamers say they leave the app for good.³ On the flip side, high-quality, user-first ad experiences actively protect engagement: 72% of gamers keep playing when ads are high quality⁴ and 20% report playing more often.⁵ These metrics demonstrate how ad quality serves as a fundamental growth engine beyond a cosmetic choice.

“App economics are not an either-or,” Sheffer said. “With quality ad experiences, users can stay engaged longer and you can naturally unlock more opportunities for other revenue streams, like in-app purchases and subscriptions.”

Sustainable revenue growth doesn’t come from aggressive ad strategies that disrupt audiences. It comes from pairing global, premium demand with uncompromising quality controls. This foundation protects in-app experiences and grows long-term revenue opportunities.

To truly scale revenue, safety cannot be restricted to a single network. Google built its advanced ad quality protections to extend across the AdMob network and participating third-party bidders. This means studios can filter out unwanted categories, block problematic creatives and apply brand rules across their bidding partners from a single interface.

Toss, a financial app company from South Korea’s Viva Republica, chose AdMob as its mediation partner to grow revenue through quality ad experiences. By using AdMob’s real-time ad review and rigorous A/B testing capabilities, Toss grew ad revenue 891% year over year while driving a 17% uplift in next-day retention for one of its app’s features.⁶

Across the wider app ecosystem, SensorTower data shows that apps primarily using AdMob’s mediation platform have a 25% higher 60-day and 90-day user retention rate than apps primarily using competing platforms.⁷˒⁸

“Our focus on quality and long-term growth explains why, in recent research, Kantar found that app developers rank Google AdMob No. 1 in both providing high-quality ads and in maximizing revenue — ahead of other leading app monetization platforms,” said Sheffer.⁹˒¹⁰

As app development cycles continue to accelerate, monetization operations must keep pace.

Instead of losing critical hours to manual creative review, app developer teams can lean on Google’s intelligent brand safety systems to help. For example, improved AI models in the Ad Review Center can swiftly flag off-brand creatives for removal. In parallel, click-through-rate-based filtering helps to block deceptive ad behaviors like forced redirects, fake buttons or hidden close icons. This tactic helps developers eliminate these formats across all bidding sources before they trigger player frustration.

For ads that need a closer look, revamped search filters in the Ad Review Center help teams find creatives faster — such as sorting creatives by revenue to prioritize the reviews that impact the bottom line.

By protecting both developer velocity and creative safety, teams can more easily grow revenue and focus on the user experience. That balance of automation and control is why a Kantar analysis showed app developers rate Google AdMob 1.5x higher than the competitor average for making it easy to monetize apps.¹¹

Delivering on ad quality and making monetization simple is only the start. Google is building new tools to help teams catch performance anomalies, implement and validate ad placements and preserve player retention.

“We believe enterprise-grade AI and monetization requires secure, native integration across the tools you already use today,” Sheffer said. “That is why Google AdMob is continuously expanding what an advanced monetization platform can do. By shifting routine diagnostics, complex yield optimizations and brand safety into intelligent, automated workflows, we are bridging the gap between app development and ad operations — so you can build, test and ship ad setups with the same speed and safety as your app code.”

Partner insights from Google Publisher Connections

Publisher Connections brings together publishers, buyers and their audiences. Across a suite of trusted platforms — from Google Ad Manager, AdMob and AdSense, to their buyer solutions — they deliver continuous innovation and powerful results.

The e-commerce giant is central for many consumer brand advertisers, but measurement changes could throw a wrench into their plans for next year.

More brands are gravitating toward live shopping, a growing channel that is set to play an outsized influence on shopping trends.

#7340. Sociable: Meta’s AI ads included unapproved discounts

Recent reports found that the artificial intelligence-powered ad tools posted discount offers to customers even with promotions turned off.

“Sociable” is the latest commentary on important social media developments and trends from industry expert Andrew Hutchinson of Social Media Today.

As artificial intelligence tools become more empowered, new parameters need to be established to limit their capacity when it comes to undertaking certain tasks.

There have already been reports of an AI tool giving out a user’s home address, generating hundreds of videos at a users’ expense and conducting questionable crypto trades.

Automated errors can also extend to buying digital ads. PPC Land reported last week that Meta’s AI-powered ad tools took discounts from a store’s website and displayed them on the business’s Facebook and Instagram ads, “even when the shop had turned promotions off on the ad it copied.”

The discovery was made by digital marketing advisor Bram Van der Hallen, who shared a LinkedIn post outlining the discovery.

As per Van der Hallen: “Meta may now automatically detect offers from your website and use them in your Meta ads. Even when you have turned off this option for your original ad. When the option to display offers detected from your website and ads is enabled in Commerce Manager, Meta may then automatically display these offers in your ads.”

Van der Hallen said the issue can be controlled by updating Commerce Manager settings to stop Meta from using detected offers in other campaigns.

But this adds another element to consider, and PPC Land noted that it could cause issues for advertisers: “This matters for any business advertising on Meta with more than one offer live, because a discount meant for one campaign or one audience could show up somewhere else.”

Meta recently outlined how its expanded processing capacity is helping generate more effective ad campaigns. The company said that improvements to its ads ranking system drove a more than 8% increase in ad clicks and a 15.7% uplift in conversions on Facebook in Q2 2026.

However, as more systems are guided by AI, there will be errors because AI systems seek out efficiencies and optimizations wherever they can, which could cause issues.

Advertisers need to be aware of these potential issues, which could have a big impact on campaign approaches.
